These two concepts are the basic concepts of the entire bill. The 10th article (c) of the bill was me, about the purpose of which personal data may be transferred to those who are obtained with legitimate interest and consent. This substance contains self-doubt and anxiety. In order to remove suspicion and anxiety, personal data that will be transferred abroad or to others must be linked to the written consent of the person concerned by the data manager before the transfer process can be made. The transfer of personal data to others must also be stated in the law as to the chain of documents, provided the search for data managers is sought at all levels from the beginning to the end. I mean, when it's clearly against the law, the board can decide whether or not it's against the law. In that case, the board would have tolerated and even rewarded the law. This statement must also be corrected in order to avoid prosecution of the board. It may also be the subject of a violation of the law by a person concerned with a matter that has been adopted and determined by the board. It is necessary to clarify this because when institutions and organisations that can transfer personal data to others belong to the public, the detection of this will be brought to administrative justice. When it belongs to the private sector, it is not explicitly stated where the claim to violation of the law will be made clear. Dear lawmakers, as it is known, law enforcement must be in accordance with the language and spirit of the law. It has been authorized to decide whether to stop the process of processing data and exporting abroad if the conditions mentioned in the 15th article (7) of the proposed bill are co-existent. The requirement for co-existence in the Article text must be rearranged in accordance with the new text, which will be the result of our motion. This is the case: The absence of consent, irreparable damage, the apparent violation of the work and the procedure, can be a separate source of the law, as well as the fact that it is difficult and impossible to compensate for. It has not been stated which of these conditions, one's existence or openness, would be a violation of the law. For the council to be free of uncertainty, to ensure the safety of the already controversial personal data and to address public concerns, the clause must be changed according to our motion. tbmm-2016-03-23sit04spe0478par0041	Mr. President, my dear friends, this bill has a fundamental problem, and in all the following articles, this fundamental problem presents itself. This bill was originally discussed within the framework of the European Union's integration legislation, so it is seeking to comply with the recommendations of the European Commission and its previous decisions. But since data collection and data processing in Turkey are essentially a process against citizens by the state's hands, its 100-year tradition, the tradition of the modern age of modernism, inevitably leads to a contradiction between the European Commission's acquisitor and state-making habits. Inevitably, this contradiction seems to be adaptable to the acquish in its form, but basically all the parameters of the habit of statehood are based on these laws. Such is the case in the 16th article. In fact, the European Commission's approach to protecting personal data is based on its record: it is based on the registration of personal data guards, not on long inspections, but on accountability. The situation that arises over complaints has been arranged to meet the request of the citizen through accountability. Yet, we are also bound by such laws but by a law that is never transparent. So the principle of setting up responsible mechanisms does not apply here. That's why we're recommending that this be taken out of the law. But if we take that out of the law, can we, along with the rest of them, reach the law that protects the citizen in the process of actual personal data? I'm 100% sure that won't happen, and you can be sure that won't. Because with all the exceptions he's brought in, the Protection of Personal Data Bill guarantees that all of the institutions that have been tagging so far are legal, that it's protected, and that it guarantees that they'll continue to tag after that. Therefore, there is no reason for citizens to rejoice that this law has been passed. In fact, to this day, all those who have printed citizens will continue to register despite the Constitution, the European Convention on Human Rights, and the laws that protect the rights of the other person in Turkey. On the other hand, with the exceptions of private organisations, we are faced with a law that, in fact, conforms to European legislation, but basically confirms the sovereignty of the state and the market over the citizens, which in fact guarantees the commercialisation of personal data, because it leaves a lot of open doors in front of the purchase and sale of personal data. So the improvement of this law is not possible unless we get rid of this fundamental contradiction, but if we can at least save this 16th article, perhaps by this motion, we may have taken it one step further. My personal opinion and the opinion of our group is that the Protection of Personal Data Bill, which the Republic of Turkey has conquered from the Ottoman State, results in the restoration of all existing mechanisms of ruling and ruling below the Ottoman state, now in a European form. Therefore, unless this fundamental contradiction is resolved, all of this will not be worth anything, unfortunately, until Turkey has reached a new constitutional regime that guarantees human rights and sees the violation of human rights as the most important human rights violation. Of course, the door to the Constitutional Court is open against this, but it is doubtful that the Constitutional Court will provide a guarantee in this regard. For all these reasons, we are opposed to the European law itself and to this clause, which is a step towards rebuilding the Ottoman state.

tbmm-2016-03-23sit07spe0531par0242	Mr. President, I have made a commitment to the Protection of Personal Data Act. The protection of personal data is perhaps one of the most sensitive areas of human rights today. Many areas of our personal lives are easily controlled in our time. With many technical opportunities, every aspect of our lives can be infiltrated, all the information of our lives, all the intimate matters that can be obtained by public authorities or third parties. Therefore, there was a need for protection, and it was decided by the Council of Europe in 1981 that this protection was needed. The Council of Europe began to work for the protection of personal data, private life and other personal rights, predicting developments. We see the results of the work done in 1981. From that time until now, the lack of legislation that contained assurances on the protection of personal data in Turkey was obviously a disgrace. Now that such a law has come to the agenda, despite its shortcomings, it is a positive step, a positive development. However, the bill that lies ahead has significant flaws and flaws. tbmm-2016-03-23sit07spe0575par0399	Mr. President, I have made a pledge on behalf of myself in the second chapter of the Protection of Personal Data Act, 117 rows under discussion. I hereby salute your delegation and our beloved nation. Dear lawmakers, personal data is all sorts of information about real people, and in this context, any data related to the person's identity information, communication, health and financial information, his personal life, his religious, political opinion is considered personal data. Both public and private institutions and organisations have been collecting personal data from time to time in connection with the fulfillment of a task or presentation of a service. For example, in the online banking transactions that we all often face, bank officials are willing to do so because of the advantages of the transaction, for security reasons, our personal data, even any letter of our mother's maiden name is required. It is normal for a business to collect information on the way a customer pays, the success and note status of an educational institution's students, the treatments of a health institution's patients, in the normal course of life. At the same time, there is and is a collection of data, which are required by law to be collected and preserved by official agencies. This is a right protected by the Constitution, as there is an absolute need for the information to be collected, processed, preserved, and used only under certain conditions and regulations by concerned and competent persons and institutions. In addition, the effects of information and communication technologies that have changed our political, social, and economic lives in a shocking way have increased and forced the need for effective protection of personal data. Our current merid legislation does not have a complete regulation on processing, preserving, and protecting personal data, but the legal regulations on this matter are in different laws. Hopefully, with this provision that we shall legislate, there will be a complete and detailed legislation for the protection of a basic human right to personal data, which is guaranteed by the Constitution. As it is known, as a result of the public vote held on September 12, 2010, the amendment to Article 20 of the Constitution states that the protection of personal data is a basic human right to maintain personal data, and the fundamental and legal procedures for the protection of personal data must be regulated. Again, a fundamental law on the protection of personal data must be enforced in order for the process of full membership in the European Union to proceed. All these statements make it necessary that the law on the protection of personal data in our country be put into effect as soon as possible. Mr. President, the esteemed lawmakers envision the establishment of the European Council's Convention 108 and its relevant directive for the implementation of principles related to the processing of personal data and the establishment of authorities that will serve independently in an independent manner to direct it. In this scope, the 19th article of the bill has been established, with the legal and financial autonomy to carry out the duties given by law, and the institution's duties with the 20th article have been established. Its annual reports on the institution's activities were aimed at the transparency and accountability of the institution, ensued to the presidency of the Turkish Parliament, the Commission for Human Rights Review and Prime Ministership. Again, the 21st article of the bill is organised by the Personal Data Protection Board to act as the institution's decision-making body. The regulation aims to allow the board to carry out its duties and powers independently under law and other legislation, and to use its powers without orders and instructions from any body, authority, authority, and person. The decision of members of the board aims to select members from a broad-based area, not seeking special conditions other than carrying general conditions for being a government official. In Article 28 of the bill, the exceptions have been set up, which are excluded from the scope of the law. In part or entirely outside the scope of the law, the matter has been carefully and carefully determined and the exceptions are considered in the text of matter. The bill is a bill that will eliminate the need for a holistic legal arrangement that has been set up in different laws over the years and is missing in our legislation. With the belief that when the draft text is passed, it will cover a significant lack of our legislation, I wish the bill to be good luck and good luck to our legal system, our nation, and I salute you all.

tbmm-2016-03-23sit07spe0606par0550	Mr. President, my fellow congressmen, I salute the General Assembly. I have taken a pledge on the 19th Amendment to the Protection of Personal Data Act. At the same time, rapidly developing communication technologies have made our world a global village, while also freeing the individual, allowing him to bond with the world. On the other hand, this technological development eliminates the privacy of the individual. In the face of technology that generates data to the most private areas of our private lives at any moment, there is really a need for a law that protects the autonomy of the individual, the privacy of private life. But it can't be protected by collecting all the data about it from genome map to race and philosophical thinking to sexual life, giving the individual a sense of constant monitoring. Therefore, the law must first maintain the privacy of private life and the autonomy of the individual. And this law provides that? Of course not. In a few ways, it is a problem for this bill to be legalized in this way. The most inconvenient aspect of this bill is the direct determination of board members to protect our personal data by the Council of Ministers and the President. Such a board cannot give society and individual the trust it expects, nor does it meet the universal criteria. As in Germany and other European countries, this council must be elected from among qualified majority experts in Parliament. If this bill is legalized, the government, which's inscribed its citizen, will be able to use all of its special data in proportion and in excess from sexual life to race, philosophy to religion and biometric data. So if this bill becomes legal, the state itself will be violating the right to protect it, because these executive staffs will make these secrets grow as they come to be. Let me give you an example of history, dear friends. Do you know what the Nazis were doing to human history? They were rounding up workers of sites, houses, apartment buildings, and they were supposed to honor them, and then they gave them an additional nation's work. Their mission was to identify the Jews, communists, socialists, democrats, Roma, even the disabled -- on their websites, apartments, neighborhoods -- and present them to the government in a list. These lists were called the black lists. There were millions of innocent people on those lists who made their voices against the war and then burned in Nazi furnaces. Many thought they were doing a national mission in good faith, and years later, when the truth came out, they couldn't face their children, their grandchildren, mankind. So, Germany and Europe, who experience this painful experiment, leave the law that protects its citizens' personal data -- their secrets -- to experts they've chosen in parliament most of the time. In fact, these chosen ones have to inform parliament of the gifts that have come to them and deliver them to parliament, and they will never be able to do any other work. Because when the device we call the government gets into the hands of anti-fascist mentalitys, instead of the secrets of the citizen, it uses its own political interest, instead of its own, as political blackmail against its citizens, instead. Because European history is filled with examples of pain, they've made the law of hairlessness. That is because the state that knows the secret of its fellowman is a righteous state. Look, how well the French philosopher Montaigne, known for his stand against colonialism, expressed this situation: I keep a secret entrusted to me as a sacred relic, but I try not to know its secrets as far as I can. I thought you said you weren't going to use the citizen to the state. I thought the secret of the citizen's privacy wasn't the government's business. In the light beam that enters this single window, with the movement of light, all the actions of the prisoner can be controlled by this one guard, but no one can be seen watching.
